def vowels_count(s: str) -> int:
    """
    Write a function vowels_count which takes a string representing
    a single word as input and returns the number of vowels in the string.
    Vowels in this case are 'a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u'. Here, 'y' is also a
    vowel, but only when it is at the end of the given word. Note, trailing
    special characters or spaces do not matter, however, numbers and letters do effect
    whether 'y' is considered to be the last letter of the word.

    Examples:
        >>> vowels_count("abcde")
        2
        >>> vowels_count(" ACEDY!")
        3
    """
    # your code here
    vowels = ["a", "e", "i", "o", "u", "y"]
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] in vowels:
            count += 1
        elif s[i] == "y":
            if i == len(s) - 1:
                count += 1
            else:
                if s[i + 1] not in vowels:
                    count += 1
    return count

def vowels_count(s: str) -> int:
    """
    Write a function vowels_count which takes a string representing
    a single word as input and returns the number of vowels in the string.
    Vowels in this case are 'a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u'. Here, 'y' is also a
    vowel, but only when it is at the end of the given word. Note, trailing
    special characters or spaces do not matter, however, numbers and letters do effect
    whether 'y' is considered to be the last letter of the word.

    Examples:
        >>> vowels_count("abcde")
        2
        >>> vowels_count(" ACEDY!")
        3
    """
    vowels = ("a", "e", "i", "o", "u")
    s = s.lower()
    vowels_count = 0
    for i in s:
        if i in vowels:
            vowels_count += 1
    return vowels_count

    If shift > number of digits, return digits reversed.

    Examples:

        "21"
        >>> circular_shift(12, 2)
        "12"

def circular_shift(x: int, shift: int) -> str:
    """
    Circular shift the digits of the integer x, shift the digits right by shift
    and return the result as a string.
    If shift > number of digits, return digits reversed.

    Examples:
        >>> circular_shift(12, 1)
        "21"
        >>> circular_shift(12, 2)
        "12"
    """
    digits = str(x)
    shift = shift % len(digits)
    return digits[shift:] + digits[:shift]

    """
    Take a string as input and return the sum of the the ASCII codes from upper characters only.

    Examples:

        digitSum("abAB") => 131
        digitSum("abcCd") => 67

def digitSum(s: str) -> int:

    Take a string as input and return the sum of the the ASCII codes from upper characters only.

def digitSum(s: str) -> int:
    """
    Take a string as input and return the sum of the the ASCII codes from upper characters only.

    Examples:
        digitSum("") => 0
        digitSum("abAB") => 131
        digitSum("abcCd") => 67
        digitSum("helloE") => 69
        digitSum("woArBld") => 131

def fruit_distribution(s: str, n: int) -> int:
    """
    In this task, you will be given a string that represents a number of apples and oranges 
    that are distributed in a basket of fruit this basket contains 
    apples, oranges, and mango fruits. Given the string, that represents the total number of 
    the oranges and apples, and an integer that represent the total number of the fruits

        fruit_distribution("2 apples and 3 oranges", 100) -> 100 - 2 - 3 = 95
        fruit_distribution("100 apples and 1 orange", 120) -> 120 - 100 - 1 = 19

def fruit_distribution(s: str, n: int) -> int:
    """
    In this task, you will be given a string that represents a number of apples and oranges 
    that are distributed in a basket of fruit this basket contains 
    apples, oranges, and mango fruits. Given the string, that represents the total number of 
    the oranges and apples, and an integer that represent the total number of the fruits 
    in the basket, return the number of the mango fruits in the basket.

    For example:
        fruit_distribution("5 apples and 6 oranges", 19) -> 19 - 5 - 6 = 8
        fruit_distribution("0 apples and 1 orange", 3) -> 3 - 0 - 1 = 2
        fruit_distribution("2 apples and 3 oranges", 100) -> 100 - 2 - 3 = 95
        fruit_distribution("100 apples and 1 orange", 120) -> 120 - 100 - 1 = 19
    """
    # 1. We find the apples and oranges
    apples = int(s.split("apples")[0])
    oranges = int(s.split("oranges")[0])

    # 2. We find the mango fruits
    mango_fruits = n - (apples + oranges)

    return mango_fruits

def fruit_distribution(s: str, n: int) -> int:
    """
    In this task, you will be given a string that represents a number of apples and oranges 
    that are distributed in a basket of fruit this basket contains 
    apples, oranges, and mango fruits. Given the string, that represents the total number of 
    the oranges and apples, and an integer that represent the total number of the fruits 
    in the basket, return the number of the mango fruits in the basket.

        fruit_distribution("5 apples and 6 oranges", 19) -> 19 - 5 - 6 = 8

        fruit_distribution("2 apples and 3 oranges", 100) -> 100 - 2 - 3 = 95
        fruit_distribution("100 apples and 1 orange", 120) -> 120 - 100 - 1 = 19

    apples, oranges = map(int, s.split(" "))
    return n - apples - oranges

def fruit_distribution(s: str, n: int) -> int:
    """
    In this task, you will be given a string that represents a number of apples and oranges 
    that are distributed in a basket of fruit this basket contains 
    apples, oranges, and mango fruits. Given the string, that represents the total number of 
    the oranges and apples, and an integer that represent the total number of the fruits 
    in the basket, return the number of the mango fruits in the basket.

    For example:
        fruit_distribution("5 apples and 6 oranges", 19) -> 19 - 5 - 6 = 8
        fruit_distribution("0 apples and 1 orange", 3) -> 3 - 0 - 1 = 2
        fruit_distribution("2 apples and 3 oranges", 100) -> 100 - 2 - 3 = 95
        fruit_distribution("100 apples and 1 orange", 120) -> 120 - 100 - 1 = 19
    """
    fruits = s.split()
    apples = int(fruits[0])
    oranges = int(fruits[2])
    mangoes = n - apples - oranges
    return mangoes

def fruit_distribution(s: str, n: int) -> int:
    """
    In this task, you will be given a string that represents a number of apples and oranges 
    that are distributed in a basket of fruit this basket contains 
    apples, oranges, and mango fruits. Given the string, that represents the total number of 
    the oranges and apples, and an integer that represent the total number of the fruits 
    in the basket, return the number of the mango fruits in the basket.

    For example:
        fruit_distribution("5 apples and 6 oranges", 19) -> 19 - 5 - 6 = 8
        fruit_distribution("0 apples and 1 orange", 3) -> 3 - 0 - 1 = 2
        fruit_distribution("2 apples and 3 oranges", 100) -> 100 - 2 - 3 = 95
        fruit_distribution("100 apples and 1 orange", 120) -> 120 - 100 - 1 = 19
    """
    apples, oranges = map(int, s.split(" ")[0].split(" "))
    return n - apples - oranges

from typing import List


def pluck(arr: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has integer nodes,
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smallest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []

    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.
    """
    if not arr:
        return []

    smallest_value = min(arr)
    smallest_index = arr.index(smallest_value)

    # find all even values in arr
    even_values = [i for i in arr if i % 2 == 0]

    # if there are no even values, return an empty array
    if not even_values:
        return []

    # find smallest even value in even_values
    smallest_even_value = min(even_values)

    # find smallest even value index in even_values
    smallest_even_index = even_values.index(smallest_even_value)

    # if smallest_even_index is smaller than smallest_index, then the smallest even value is in arr
    if smallest_even_index < smallest_index:
        return [smallest_even_value, smallest_even_index]
    else:
        return [smallest_value, smallest_index]

from typing import List


def pluck(arr: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has integer nodes,
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smallest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []

    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.
    """
    if not arr:
        return []

    even_min = float("inf")
    even_min_index = None

    for i, n in enumerate(arr):
        if n % 2 == 0 and n < even_min:
            even_min = n
            even_min_index = i

    return [even_min, even_min_index] if even_min_index is not None else []

from typing import List


def pluck(arr: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has integer nodes,
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smallest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []

    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.
    """
    if not arr:
        return []
    smallest = min([i for i in arr if i % 2 == 0])
    return [smallest, arr.index(smallest)]

from typing import List


def pluck(arr: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has integer nodes,
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smallest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []

    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.
    """
    if len(arr) == 0:
        return []

    # Find the smallest even value in the array
    smallest_value = None
    for i in range(len(arr)):
        if arr[i] % 2 == 0 and (smallest_value is None or arr[i] < smallest_value):
            smallest_value = arr[i]

    if smallest_value is None:
        return []

    # Find the index of the smallest even value
    smallest_index = None
    for i in range(len(arr)):
        if arr[i] == smallest_value and (smallest_index is None or i < smallest_index):
            smallest_index = i

    return [smallest_value, smallest_index]

from typing import List


def pluck(arr: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has integer nodes,
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smallest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []

    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.
    """
    if not arr:
        return []

    min_index = 0
    min_value = arr[0]

    for i in range(1, len(arr)):
        if arr[i] <= min_value:
            min_value = arr[i]
            min_index = i

    if min_value % 2 == 0:
        return [min_value, min_index]

    return []

from typing import List


def search(lst: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    You are given a non-empty list of positive integers. Return the greatest integer that is greater than 
    zero, and has a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself. 
    The frequency of an integer is the number of times it appears in the list.
    If no such a value exist, return -1.
    
    Examples:
        search([4, 1, 2, 2, 3, 1]) == 2
        search([1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4]) == 3
        search([5, 5, 4, 4, 4]) == -1
    """
    if not lst:
        return -1

    max_num = lst[0]
    max_freq = 1
    for i in range(1, len(lst)):
        if lst[i] > max_num:
            max_num = lst[i]
        if lst[i] == max_num:
            max_freq += 1
        if max_freq >= max_num:
            return max_num

    return -1

from typing import List


def search(lst: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    You are given a non-empty list of positive integers. Return the greatest integer that is greater than 
    zero, and has a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself. 
    The frequency of an integer is the number of times it appears in the list.
    If no such a value exist, return -1.
    
    Examples:
        search([4, 1, 2, 2, 3, 1]) == 2
        search([1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4]) == 3
        search([5, 5, 4, 4, 4]) == -1
    """
    count = 0
    max_count = 0
    result = -1
    for i in range(len(lst)):
        if lst[i] > 0:
            count = 1
            for j in range(i + 1, len(lst)):
                if lst[i] == lst[j]:
                    count += 1
            if count >= lst[i]:
                if count > max_count:
                    max_count = count
                    result = lst[i]
    return result

from typing import List


def search(lst: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    You are given a non-empty list of positive integers. Return the greatest integer that is greater than 
    zero, and has a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself. 
    The frequency of an integer is the number of times it appears in the list.
    If no such a value exist, return -1.
    
    Examples:
        search([4, 1, 2, 2, 3, 1]) == 2
        search([1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4]) == 3
        search([5, 5, 4, 4, 4]) == -1
    """
    freq = {}
    for num in lst:
        if num not in freq:
            freq[num] = 0
        freq[num] += 1

    if len(freq) == 1:
        return -1

    for num in sorted(freq.keys(), reverse=True):
        if freq[num] >= num:
            return num

    return -1

from typing import List


def strange_sort_list(lst: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given a list of integers, return the list in a 'strange' order.
    Strange sorting, is when you start with the minimum value,
    then the maximum value from the remaining integers, then the minimum and so on.

    Examples:
        strange_sort_list([1, 2, 3, 4]) == [1, 4, 2, 3]
        strange_sort_list([5, 5, 5, 5]) == [5, 5, 5, 5]
        strange_sort_list([]) == []
    """

from typing import List


def strange_sort_list(lst: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given a list of integers, return the list in a 'strange' order.
    Strange sorting, is when you start with the minimum value,
    then the maximum value from the remaining integers, then the minimum and so on.

    Examples:
        strange_sort_list([1, 2, 3, 4]) == [1, 4, 2, 3]
        strange_sort_list([5, 5, 5, 5]) == [5, 5, 5, 5]
        strange_sort_list([]) == []
    """
    if len(lst) == 0:
        return []
    res = [lst[0]]
    while lst:
        if len(lst) == 1:
            return res + lst
        if len(lst) == 2:
            return res + lst
        lst.remove(max(lst))
        res.append(max(lst))
    return res

from typing import List


def strange_sort_list(lst: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given a list of integers, return the list in a 'strange' order.
    Strange sorting, is when you start with the minimum value,
    then the maximum value from the remaining integers, then the minimum and so on.

    Examples:
        strange_sort_list([1, 2, 3, 4]) == [1, 4, 2, 3]
        strange_sort_list([5, 5, 5, 5]) == [5, 5, 5, 5]
        strange_sort_list([]) == []
    """
    if len(lst) == 0:
        return []
    min_val = min(lst)
    max_val = max(lst)
    sorted_list = [min_val, max_val]
    lst.remove(min_val)
    lst.remove(max_val)
    return [min_val, max_val] + strange_sort_list(lst)

--------------------------------------------------
from typing import List


def strange_sort_list(lst: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given a list of integers, return the list in a 'strange' order.
    Strange sorting, is when you start with the minimum value,
    then the maximum value from the remaining integers, then the minimum and so on.

    Examples:
        strange_sort_list([1, 2, 3, 4]) == [1, 4, 2, 3]
        strange_sort_list([5, 5, 5, 5]) == [5, 5, 5, 5]
        strange_sort_list([]) == []
    """
    if not lst:
        return []

    # If there are even number of elements, then we have to choose the middle one
    # If there are odd number of elements, then we can choose any element
    # from the middle.
    middle = len(lst) // 2 if len(lst) % 2 == 0 else len(lst) // 2 + 1

    return sorted(lst, key=lambda x: (x >= middle, x))

from typing import List


def strange_sort_list(lst: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    """
    Given a list of integers, return the list in a 'strange' order.
    Strange sorting, is when you start with the minimum value,
    then the maximum value from the remaining integers, then the minimum and so on.

    Examples:
        strange_sort_list([1, 2, 3, 4]) == [1, 4, 2, 3]
        strange_sort_list([5, 5, 5, 5]) == [5, 5, 5, 5]
        strange_sort_list([]) == []
    """
    if not lst:
        return lst

    result = []
    while lst:
        min_value = min(lst)
        result.append(min_value)
        lst.remove(min_value)
        if lst:
            max_value = max(lst)
            result.append(max_value)
            lst.remove(max_value)
    return result

def triangle_area(a: int, b: int, c: int) -> float:
    """
    Given the lengths of the three sides of a triangle. Return the area of
    the triangle rounded to 2 decimal points if the three sides form a valid triangle. 
    Otherwise return -1
    Three sides make a valid triangle when the sum of any two sides is greater 
    than the third side.

    Examples:
        triangle_area(3, 4, 5) == 6.00
        triangle_area(1, 2, 10) == -1
    """
    if a + b <= c or a + c <= b or b + c <= a:
        return -1
    s = (a + b + c) / 2
    area = (s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c)) ** (1 / 2)
    return area

from typing import List


def will_it_fly(q: List[int], w: int) -> bool:
    """
    Write a function that returns True if the object q will fly, and False otherwise.
    The object q will fly if it's balanced (it is a palindromic list) and the sum of its elements is less than or equal the maximum possible weight w.
    If the requirements are not met, the object will not fly.
    
    Other requirements:
        * w is a non-negative integer
        * q is a list of non-negative integers
        * q is a connected object, i.e. no zeros between non-zero elements

    Examples:
        will_it_fly([1, 2], 5) == False 
        (1 + 2 is less than the maximum possible weight, but it's unbalanced)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 1) == False
        (it's balanced, but 3 + 2 + 3 is more than the maximum possible weight)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 9) == True
        (3 + 2 + 3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced)

        will_it_fly([0, 3], 5) == True
        (3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced since the 0 is negligible for the balance)
    """

from typing import List


def will_it_fly(q: List[int], w: int) -> bool:
    """
    Write a function that returns True if the object q will fly, and False otherwise.
    The object q will fly if it's balanced (it is a palindromic list) and the sum of its elements is less than or equal the maximum possible weight w.
    If the requirements are not met, the object will not fly.
    
    Other requirements:
        * w is a non-negative integer
        * q is a list of non-negative integers
        * q is a connected object, i.e. no zeros between non-zero elements

    Examples:
        will_it_fly([1, 2], 5) == False 
        (1 + 2 is less than the maximum possible weight, but it's unbalanced)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 1) == False
        (it's balanced, but 3 + 2 + 3 is more than the maximum possible weight)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 9) == True
        (3 + 2 + 3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced)

        will_it_fly([0, 3], 5) == True
        (3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced since the 0 is negligible for the balance)
    """
    if sum(q) <= w:
        if q == q[::-1]:
            return True
    return False

from typing import List


def will_it_fly(q: List[int], w: int) -> bool:
    """
    Write a function that returns True if the object q will fly, and False otherwise.
    The object q will fly if it's balanced (it is a palindromic list) and the sum of its elements is less than or equal the maximum possible weight w.
    If the requirements are not met, the object will not fly.
    
    Other requirements:
        * w is a non-negative integer
        * q is a list of non-negative integers
        * q is a connected object, i.e. no zeros between non-zero elements

    Examples:
        will_it_fly([1, 2], 5) == False 
        (1 + 2 is less than the maximum possible weight, but it's unbalanced)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 1) == False
        (it's balanced, but 3 + 2 + 3 is more than the maximum possible weight)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 9) == True
        (3 + 2 + 3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced)

        will_it_fly([0, 3], 5) == True
        (3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced since the 0 is negligible for the balance)
    """
    if w < sum(q):
        return False
    return q == q[::-1]

from typing import List


def will_it_fly(q: List[int], w: int) -> bool:
    """
    Write a function that returns True if the object q will fly, and False otherwise.
    The object q will fly if it's balanced (it is a palindromic list) and the sum of its elements is less than or equal the maximum possible weight w.
    If the requirements are not met, the object will not fly.
    
    Other requirements:
        * w is a non-negative integer
        * q is a list of non-negative integers
        * q is a connected object, i.e. no zeros between non-zero elements

    Examples:
        will_it_fly([1, 2], 5) == False 
        (1 + 2 is less than the maximum possible weight, but it's unbalanced)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 1) == False
        (it's balanced, but 3 + 2 + 3 is more than the maximum possible weight)

        will_it_fly([0, 3], 5) == True
        (3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced since the 0 is negligible for the balance)
    """

def will_it_fly(q: List[int], w: int) -> bool:
    """
    Write a function that returns True if the object q will fly, and False otherwise.
    The object q will fly if it's balanced (it is a palindromic list) and the sum of its elements is less than or equal the maximum possible weight w.
    If the requirements are not met, the object will not fly.
    
    Other requirements:
        * w is a non-negative integer
        * q is a list of non-negative integers
        * q is a connected object, i.e. no zeros between non-zero elements

    Examples:
        will_it_fly([1, 2], 5) == False 
        (1 + 2 is less than the maximum possible weight, but it's unbalanced)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 1) == False
        (it's balanced, but 3 + 2 + 3 is more than the maximum possible weight)

        will_it_fly([3, 2, 3], 9) == True
        (3 + 2 + 3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced)

        will_it_fly([0, 3], 5) == True
        (3 is less than the maximum possible weight, and it's balanced since the 0 is negligible for the balance)
    """
    # return w >= sum(q) and q == q[::-1]
    return sum(q) <= w and q == q[::-1]

--------------------------------------------------
from typing import List


def smallest_change(arr: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    Given an array arr of integers, find the minimum number of elements that
    need to be changed to make the array palindromic. A palindromic array is an array that
    is read the same backwards and forwards. In one change, you can change one element to any other element.

    For example:
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 5, 4, 7, 9, 6]) == 4
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2, 2]) == 1
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 2, 1]) == 0
    """
    length = len(arr)
    if length <= 1:
        return 0

    # store the number of times each number appears
    # for example, [1, 2, 3, 3, 2, 1] -> {1: 2, 2: 2, 3: 2}
    number_counts = {}
    for i in arr:
        if i not in number_counts:
            number_counts[i] = 1
        else:
            number_counts[i] += 1

    # store the number of times each number appears in pairs
    # for example, {1: 2, 2: 2, 3: 2} -> {1: 1, 2: 2, 3: 1}
    pair_counts = number_counts.copy()
    for key in number_counts:
        pair_counts[key] = number_counts[key] // 2

    # find the number of elements that are not in pairs
    # for example, {1: 1, 2: 2, 3: 1} -> {3: 1}
    unpaired_counts = {}
    for key in number_counts:
        if key not in pair_counts:
            unpaired_counts[key] = number_counts[key]

    # find the smallest number that can be changed to make the array palindromic
    smallest_change = 0
    for key in unpaired_counts:
        smallest_change += unpaired_counts[key]

    # find the smallest number of changes that can be made to make the array palindromic
    smallest_change += len(pair_counts) - 1

    return smallest_change

--------------------------------------------------
from typing import List


def smallest_change(arr: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    Given an array arr of integers, find the minimum number of elements that
    need to be changed to make the array palindromic. A palindromic array is an array that
    is read the same backwards and forwards. In one change, you can change one element to any other element.

    For example:
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 5, 4, 7, 9, 6]) == 4
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2, 2]) == 1
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 2, 1]) == 0
    """
    if len(arr) < 2:
        return 0

    if is_palindrome(arr):
        return 0

    min_value = min(arr)
    max_value = max(arr)

    palindrome_arr = sorted(arr, reverse=True)

    max_palindrome_index = palindrome_arr.index(max_value)
    min_palindrome_index = palindrome_arr.index(min_value)

    return min(
        max_palindrome_index - min_palindrome_index,
        len(arr) - max_palindrome_index - min_palindrome_index - 1
    )

--------------------------------------------------
from typing import List


def smallest_change(arr: List[int]) -> int:
    """
    Given an array arr of integers, find the minimum number of elements that
    need to be changed to make the array palindromic. A palindromic array is an array that
    is read the same backwards and forwards. In one change, you can change one element to any other element.

    For example:
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 5, 4, 7, 9, 6]) == 4
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2, 2]) == 1
        smallest_change([1, 2, 3, 2, 1]) == 0
    """
    if not arr:
        return 0

    count = 0
    for i in range(len(arr) // 2):
        if arr[i] != arr[~i]:
            count += 1

    return count

from typing import List


def total_match(lst1: List[str], lst2: List[str]) -> List[str]:
    """
    Write a function that accepts two lists of strings and returns the list that has 
    less total number of chars in the all strings of the list compared to the other list.

    If the two lists have the same number of chars, return the first list.

    Examples:
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hI', 'Hi']) -> ['hI', 'Hi']
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hi', 'hi', 'admin', 'project']) -> ['hi', 'admin']
    """
    # First, we need to count the total number of characters in each list
    # Then, we will compare the total number of characters of each list
    lst1_total = sum([len(item) for item in lst1])
    lst2_total = sum([len(item) for item in lst2])

    if lst1_total == lst2_total:
        return lst1
    elif lst1_total < lst2_total:
        return lst1
    else:
        return lst2

from typing import List


def total_match(lst1: List[str], lst2: List[str]) -> List[str]:
    """
    Write a function that accepts two lists of strings and returns the list that has 
    less total number of chars in the all strings of the list compared to the other list.

    If the two lists have the same number of chars, return the first list.

    Examples:
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hI', 'Hi']) -> ['hI', 'Hi']
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hi', 'hi', 'admin', 'project']) -> ['hi', 'admin']
    """
    total_chars_1 = sum([len(word) for word in lst1])
    total_chars_2 = sum([len(word) for word in lst2])

    if total_chars_1 == total_chars_2:
        return lst1
    elif total_chars_1 < total_chars_2:
        return lst1
    else:
        return lst2

from typing import List


def total_match(lst1: List[str], lst2: List[str]) -> List[str]:
    """
    Write a function that accepts two lists of strings and returns the list that has 
    less total number of chars in the all strings of the list compared to the other list.

    If the two lists have the same number of chars, return the first list.

    Examples:
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hI', 'Hi']) -> ['hI', 'Hi']
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hi', 'hi', 'admin', 'project']) -> ['hi', 'admin']
    """
    if not lst1 or not lst2:
        return []
    lst1_count, lst2_count = 0, 0
    for i in lst1:
        lst1_count += len(i)
    for i in lst2:
        lst2_count += len(i)
    return lst1 if lst1_count < lst2_count else lst2

from typing import List


def total_match(lst1: List[str], lst2: List[str]) -> List[str]:
    """
    Write a function that accepts two lists of strings and returns the list that has 
    less total number of chars in the all strings of the list compared to the other list.

    If the two lists have the same number of chars, return the first list.

    Examples:
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hI', 'Hi']) -> ['hI', 'Hi']
        total_match(['hi', 'admin'], ['hi', 'hi', 'admin', 'project']) -> ['hi', 'admin']
    """
    # 1. Calculate total number of chars in each list
    lst1_total_chars = sum([len(i) for i in lst1])
    lst2_total_chars = sum([len(i) for i in lst2])

    # 2. Return the first list if there is a match
    if lst1_total_chars == lst2_total_chars:
        return lst1

    # 3. Return the list with less number of chars
    return lst1 if lst1_total_chars < lst2_total_chars else lst2

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """
    # 1. Base case: if n is 1, then x is a simple power of n.
    if n == 1:
        return x == 1
    # 2. Recursive case: if x is a power of n, x % n is a simple power of n.
    if x % n == 0:
        return is_simple_power(x // n, n)
    # 3. If none of the above conditions is satisfied, x is not a simple power of n.
    return False

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """

        return False
    if n == 1:
        return True
    if x == 1:
        return False
    if x == n:
        return True
    if x > n:
        return False
    return is_simple_power(n, n)

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """
    if x == n:
        return True
    for i in range(2, x):
        if n ** i == x:
            return True
    return False

BODY REPR
--------------------------------------------------
'x_power = 1\n while x_power <= x:\n     if x_power == x:\n         return True\n     x_power *= n\n return False'

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """
    x_power = 1
    while x_power <= x:
        if x_power == x:
            return True
        x_power *= n
    return False

'if n == 1:\n     return x == 1\n if x == n:\n     return True\n if x % n != 0:\n     return False\n return is_simple_power(x // n, n)'

     return x == 1
 if x == n:
     return True
 if x % n != 0:
     return False
 return is_simple_power(x // n, n)

def is_simple_power(x: int, n: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number x is a simple power of n.
    x is a simple power of n if n to the power of some integer equals x.
    For example:
        is_simple_power(1, 4) => true (4^0 = 1)
        is_simple_power(2, 2) => true (2^1 = 2)
        is_simple_power(8, 2) => true (2^3 = 8)
        is_simple_power(3, 2) => false (only 2 and 4 are neighbouring simple powers)
        is_simple_power(3, 1) => false (1 is not a simple power of any number besides itself)
    """
    if n == 1:
        return x == 1
    if x == n:
        return True
    if x % n != 0:
        return False
    return is_simple_power(x // n, n)

def iscube(a: int) -> bool:
    """
    Check if number a is a cube of some integer number.
    
    Examples:
        iscube(2) == False
        iscube(64) == True
        iscube(-180) == False
    """
    if a == 0:
        return True
    elif a > 0:
        return a**(1/3) == int(a**(1/3))
    else:
        return False

def hex_key(num: str) -> int:
    """
    Based on a hexadecimal number, received as a string,
    counts the number of hexadecimal digits that are primes.

    You may assume the input is always correct or empty string, 
    and symbols A,B,C,D,E,F are always uppercase.

    Examples:
        For num = "AB" the output should be 1.
        For num = "2020" the output should be 2.
    """
    num = int(num, 16)
    result = 0

    while num != 1:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            num //= 2
        elif num % 3 == 0:
            num //= 3
        elif num % 5 == 0:
            num //= 5
        elif num % 7 == 0:
            num //= 7
        elif num % 11 == 0:
            num //= 11
        elif num % 13 == 0:
            num //= 13
        elif num % 17 == 0:
            num //= 17
        else:
            break
        result += 1

    return result

EXTRACTED FUNCTION | SAMPLE 5
--------------------------------------------------
def hex_key(num: str) -> int:
    """
    Based on a hexadecimal number, received as a string,
    counts the number of hexadecimal digits that are primes.

    You may assume the input is always correct or empty string, 
    and symbols A,B,C,D,E,F are always uppercase.

    Examples:
        For num = "AB" the output should be 1.
        For num = "2020" the output should be 2.
    """
    hex_dict = {"A":1, "B":2, "C":3, "D":4, "E":5, "F":6}
    hex_num = []
    for char in num:
        hex_num.append(hex_dict[char])
    hex_num.sort()
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(hex_num)):
        if hex_num[i] > 1:
            for j in range(i+1,len(hex_num)):
                if hex_num[i] % hex_num[j] == 0:
                    break
                if hex_num[j] % hex_num[i] == 0:
                    count += 1
                    break
        elif hex_num[i] == 1:
            count += 1
    return count

    """
    Check if the string is happy or not:
    A string is happy if its length is at least 3 and every 3 consecutive letters are distinct.

    Examples:
      is_happy(a) => False
      is_happy(aa) => False
      is_happy(abcd) => True
      is_happy(aabb) => False

      is_happy(aabb) => False

def is_happy(s: str) -> bool:
    """
    Check if the string is happy or not:
    A string is happy if its length is at least 3 and every 3 consecutive letters are distinct.

    Examples:
      is_happy(a) => False
      is_happy(aa) => False
      is_happy(abcd) => True
      is_happy(aabb) => False

from typing import List


def numerical_letter_grade(grades: List[float]) -> List[str]:
    """
    It is the last week of the semester and the teacher has to give the grades
    to students. The teacher has been making her own algorithm for grading.
    The only problem is, she has lost the code she used for grading.
    She has given you a list of one decimal place GPAs for some students and you have to write 
    a function that can output a list of letter grades using the following table:
             GPA       |    Letter grade
              4.0                A+
            > 3.7                A 
            > 3.3                A- 
            > 3.0                B+
            > 2.7                B 
            > 2.3                B-
            > 2.0                C+
            > 1.7                C
            > 1.3                C-
            > 1.0                D+ 
            > 0.7                D 
            > 0.0                D-
              0.0                E
    
    For input out of bounds, return an empty string.
    Also, the grade needs to be rounded (half up) to 1 decimal place if needed,
    before being converted to a letter grade. For edge scenarios, consider a tolerance of 1e-6.

    Example:
        grade_equation([4.0, 3, 1.72, 2, 3.5, 4.5]) ==> ['A+', 'B', 'C-', 'C', 'A-', '']
    """

    to students. The teacher has been making her own algorithm for grading.
    The only problem is, she has lost the code she used for grading.

            > 3.7                A

            > 3.0                B+
            > 2.7                B 
            > 2.3                B-

            > 1.3                C-
            > 1.0                D+

              0.0                E

    before being converted to a letter grade. For edge scenarios, consider a tolerance of 1e-6.

    Example:
        grade_equation([4.0, 3, 1.72, 2, 3.5, 4.5]) ==> ['A+', 'B', 'C-', 'C', 'A-', '']
    """
    return [letter_grade(grade) for grade in grades]

--------------------------------------------------
from typing import List


def numerical_letter_grade(grades: List[float]) -> List[str]:
    """
    It is the last week of the semester and the teacher has to give the grades
    to students. The teacher has been making her own algorithm for grading.
    The only problem is, she has lost the code she used for grading.
    She has given you a list of one decimal place GPAs for some students and you have to write 
    a function that can output a list of letter grades using the following table:
             GPA       |    Letter grade
              4.0                A+
            > 3.7                A 
            > 3.3                A- 
            > 3.0                B+
            > 2.7                B 
            > 2.3                B-
            > 2.0                C+
            > 1.7                C
            > 1.3                C-
            > 1.0                D+ 
            > 0.7                D 
            > 0.0                D-
              0.0                E
    
    For input out of bounds, return an empty string.
    Also, the grade needs to be rounded (half up) to 1 decimal place if needed,
    before being converted to a letter grade. For edge scenarios, consider a tolerance of 1e-6.

    Example:
        grade_equation([4.0, 3, 1.72, 2, 3.5, 4.5]) ==> ['A+', 'B', 'C-', 'C', 'A-', '']
    """
    gpa_to_letter_grade_mapping = {
        4.0: "A+",
        3.7: "A",
        3.3: "A-",
        3.0: "B+",
        2.7: "B",
        2.3: "B-",
        2.0: "C+",
        1.7: "C",
        1.3: "C-",
        1.0: "D+",
        0.7: "D",
        0.0: "E",
    }
    return [
        gpa_to_letter_grade_mapping[round(gpa, 1)] if gpa in gpa_to_letter_grade_mapping else ""
        for gpa in grades
    ]

from typing import List


def numerical_letter_grade(grades: List[float]) -> List[str]:
    """
    It is the last week of the semester and the teacher has to give the grades
    to students. The teacher has been making her own algorithm for grading.
    The only problem is, she has lost the code she used for grading.
    She has given you a list of one decimal place GPAs for some students and you have to write 
    a function that can output a list of letter grades using the following table:
             GPA       |    Letter grade
              4.0                A+
            > 3.7                A 
            > 3.3                A- 
            > 3.0                B+
            > 2.7                B 
            > 2.3                B-
            > 2.0                C+
            > 1.7                C
            > 1.3                C-
            > 1.0                D+ 
            > 0.7                D 
            > 0.0                D-
              0.0                E
    
    For input out of bounds, return an empty string.
    Also, the grade needs to be rounded (half up) to 1 decimal place if needed,
    before being converted to a letter grade. For edge scenarios, consider a tolerance of 1e-6.

    Example:
        grade_equation([4.0, 3, 1.72, 2, 3.5, 4.5]) ==> ['A+', 'B', 'C-', 'C', 'A-', '']
    """

from typing import List


def numerical_letter_grade(grades: List[float]) -> List[str]:
    """
    It is the last week of the semester and the teacher has to give the grades
    to students. The teacher has been making her own algorithm for grading.
    The only problem is, she has lost the code she used for grading.
    She has given you a list of one decimal place GPAs for some students and you have to write 
    a function that can output a list of letter grades using the following table:
             GPA       |    Letter grade
              4.0                A+
            > 3.7                A 
            > 3.3                A- 
            > 3.0                B+
            > 2.7                B 
            > 2.3                B-
            > 2.0                C+
            > 1.7                C
            > 1.3                C-
            > 1.0                D+ 
            > 0.7                D 
            > 0.0                D-
              0.0                E
    
    For input out of bounds, return an empty string.
    Also, the grade needs to be rounded (half up) to 1 decimal place if needed,
    before being converted to a letter grade. For edge scenarios, consider a tolerance of 1e-6.

    Example:
        grade_equation([4.0, 3, 1.72, 2, 3.5, 4.5]) ==> ['A+', 'B', 'C-', 'C', 'A-', '']
    """
    grades.sort()
    #
